Amund Bergland Kvalsvik


Haunt Me No Longer!
Finding Meaningful Solutions
to Speculative Side-Channel
Attacks



Thesis for the degree of Philosophiae Doctor

Trondheim, July 2025

Norwegian University of Science and Technology
Faculty of Information Technology and Electrical Engineering
Department of Computer Science
                                  Abstract

Computers lie at the heart of modern society, and are relied upon for secure
and efficient computing. However, modern processor designs are vulnerable
to speculative side-channel attacks, which exploit speculation and inadvertent
sources of information leakage to attack the security of computing at its core.
Secure speculation schemes aim to mitigate these attacks, and will be key in
stopping speculative side-channel attacks. Meaningful solutions – solutions
which effectively mitigate relevant attacks at limited costs – become more
important as the field matures.
   This thesis documents five research contributions which aim to advance the
field of secure speculation schemes. Our first contribution is Doppelganger
Loads, a method by which blocked loads can securely issue under secure
speculation schemes. Using address prediction, we break dangerous data
dependencies, restore memory-level parallelism, and improve performance.
   Our second contribution is the Reveal/Conceal (ReCon) paradigm, which
efficiently tracks previously leaked data through the cache and removes se-
curity protections for such data. This enables more instructions to execute,
improving performance, at no security risk, as data has previously leaked.
   Our third research contribution is ReConnaissance, a design which correctly
applies the ReCon paradigm for multiple secret data sources. ReCon risks
leaking data when an instruction depends on multiple secret data sources,
but only one such source is tracked. ReConnaissance addresses this, and
prevents data leakage while preserving most of the performance benefit.
   Our fourth research contribution is effective microarchitectures for two
state-of-the-art secure speculation schemes. These hardware realizable de-
signs uncover previous oversights, remedies them, and showcases alternative
applications of secure speculation techniques.
   Our final research contribution is better insight into the potential perfor-
mance and resource costs of secure speculation schemes, by evaluating timing,
area, and power results. This analysis additionally highlights performance
trends for more parallel and higher performance systems.
Structure of Thesis
This thesis is a collection of papers organized into two parts.
  Part I provides an overview of the research contributions, introduces rele-
vant background, and discusses the state of the field.
  Part II includes the published and submitted papers. For improved read-
ability, the formatting of the papers has been altered, but their content
remains unchanged. The four papers included in this thesis are listed below.
Paper A. Doppelganger Loads: A Safe, Complexity-Effective Opti-
         mization for Secure Speculation Schemes
         Amund Bergland Kvalsvik, Pavlos Aimoniotis, Stefanos Kaxiras,
         Magnus Själander
         50th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture
         (ISCA), 2023
         HiPEAC Paper Award
Paper B. ReCon: Efficient Detection, Management, and Use of
         Non-Speculative Information Leakage

                                                                             xiii
      Part I

Research Overview




                    1
Chapter 1
Introduction
Computers originally came about as expedient compute units, powerful ma-
chines that could trivialize calculations which were previously burdensome
to do by hand. The scope of the computer quickly expanded beyond their
mathematical origins, and computers are now ubiquitous in nearly all facets
of modern society. In the preceding half century, the processing power of
computers has increased by orders of magnitude. Moore’s Law famously cor-
rectly predicted an exponential increase in transistors [66], which has enabled
more complex microarchitectures, giving massive performance gains [12].
   As computers have become ubiquitous in modern society, we rely greatly
on them for many key aspects of our daily lives, including privacy-sensitive
operations. Great effort is invested into ensuring that computers operate
as intended, and that sensitive data and operations are safeguarded from
malicious interference and data leakage. However, these security efforts rely
on the core design of computers being secure. If this core is vulnerable, it
risks threatening the entire security ecosystem of computing.


1.1 Motivation
During the growth of computer processing power, computers also became
much more complex, employing advanced processor designs that utilize many
different optimizations. These designs and optimizations help hide perfor-
mance limiters such as differences in logic and memory latency [82], but
commonly introduce new side-channels, inadvertent sources of information
leakage. Side-channels, stemming both from design features and physical
design characteristics, can be exploited in attacks to infer information about
program execution and behavior. Such attacks are known as side-channel
attacks and attack the core of computer design [54].
   Though side-channels have been a known security risk for modern proces-
sors for many years, they have been difficult to eliminate, due to how closely
intertwined they are with core design features. Side-channel attacks grew



                                                                             3
Chapter 1 Introduction


greatly in notoriety with the release of Spectre and Meltdown [43, 50], which
showed how side-channel attacks could be augmented through the use of
speculation. These new speculative side-channel attacks are more powerful
than conventional side-channel attacks, with some variants able to steal se-
cret data from anywhere in memory. Speculative side-channel attacks quickly
became an active field of research, with many novel attacks and variants
being created over the years [1, 2, 9, 10, 15, 16, 19, 22, 58, 61, 67, 68, 74, 77,
81]. As with conventional side-channel attacks, comprehensively eliminating
these attacks have proven difficult, due to their exploitation of fundamental
design aspects of modern computer design.
   Secure speculation schemes have been developed in attempts to mitigate
speculative side-channel attacks [5–7, 23, 38, 39, 41, 52, 62, 63, 72, 78, 80,
84, 89]. They vary greatly in strategy and scope, some of them aiming to
comprehensively mitigate all speculative side-channel attacks, while others
aim only to mitigate the most easily exploitable and powerful of the side-
channel attacks. Evaluating the efficiency of various schemes is complex, as
there are a multitude of factors to consider, such as overall performance,
resource cost, and design complexity. Similarly, as the schemes mitigate
different classes of speculative side-channel attacks, it is also necessary to
evaluate which attacks to prioritize defending against.


1.2 Mission Statement
As research matures, and industry looks to adopt leading strategies, it be-
comes important to find meaningful solutions to speculative side-channel
attacks, solutions which effectively mitigate speculative side-channel attacks
at limited costs in terms of resources and performance. These solutions must
not only be theoretically sound, but also realizable in hardware, or else they
risk incurring considerable additional design costs during implementation.
Accurately evaluating the costs of secure speculation schemes, providing effec-
tive microarchitectures based on hardware realizable designs, and converging
on reasonable strategies are chief priorities for the field.
   As the performance cost of secure speculation schemes can be severe, it
is also beneficial to consider potential optimization strategies [3, 45, 76, 90,
91]. Many different such strategies have been developed over the years as
a support pillar for secure schemes, offering performance improvement or
effectivizing previously existing designs. These optimization strategies can
exploit previously leaked data, invisible operations, instruction reordering, or
other approaches, in order to limit the impact of secure speculation schemes.



4
                                                  1.3 Research Contributions


Critically, optimization strategies must be carefully developed so at to not
weaken the security guarantees of the underlying secure speculation scheme,
and thereby invalidate the original premise.


1.3 Research Contributions
This thesis comprises five years of research within speculative side-channel
attacks, secure speculation schemes, and secure speculation optimizations.
Through this research we hope to further our understanding of secure specula-
tion schemes, their limitations and costs, potential microarchitectural designs,
and generalizable optimization strategies. In total, we present five research
contributions that we have achieved through this thesis, and their premise.
   Our research initially concerned itself with finding promising optimization
strategies. Our first contribution is the usage of address prediction in order
to break dangerous dependency chains. Within secure speculation schemes,
loads which depend on loads are commonly restricted from executing, as they
are a prime vehicle for data leakage. Restricting loads in this way incurs
a heavy performance penalty as memory-level parallelism is key for high
performance. However, as we show in Paper A: Doppelganger Loads,
it is possible to utilize the address histories of load instructions, in order
to predict the addresses of future loads in a secure manner. The benefit of
this is that the predicted address can generate a Doppelganger Load, which
has no dependency on potentially dangerous data, and can securely issue,
improving performance for secure speculation schemes notably. Our first
research contribution is therefore: A novel usage of address prediction
to regain performance for secure speculation schemes, see Section 4.1.
   We also explored other methods of exploiting past execution to improve
the performance of secure speculation schemes. Data which has already
leaked as part of non-speculative execution does not need to be protected
in the future under speculative execution. Loads which depend on older
loads commonly leak information about the older loads through accesses to
the memory hierarchy. In such load pairs, we say that the second loads
can leak the data from the memory location of the first load, and in such
cases, security restrictions can be disabled for that data, as it has already
leaked. We formalize a paradigm we call Reveal/Conceal (ReCon), which
tracks such leakage and removes protections for secure schemes for leaked
data, in order to improve the performance of secure speculation schemes. We
explore this paradigm in detail, together with potential considerations for
multi-core systems in Paper B: ReCon, providing meaningful performance



                                                                              5
Chapter 1 Introduction


improvements at a low cost for secure speculation schemes. This is our second
research contribution: A novel usage of non-speculative data leakage
to remove secure speculation protections for improved performance,
see Section 4.2.
   Later on, as we focused more on detailing secure speculation schemes, we
found a weakness in ReCon, which could lead to potential data leakage. While
allowing leaked data sources to execute freely is secure, these data sources
must correlate exactly to all the data dependencies of a given instruction. In
cases where an instruction has multiple data dependencies, all such depen-
dencies must be revealed for it to be safe to execute the instruction early. In
Paper C: ReConnaissance, we show that when combined with schemes
that track only a single secret dependency, ReCon introduces data leakage by
allowing instructions to execute before all secret dependencies are secure. We
present ReConnaissance, which addresses this oversight, and correctly marks
instructions with multiple secret dependencies, preventing them from leaking
secret data. This is our third research contribution: Secure applications
of Reveal/Conceal paradigms for multiple secret dependencies.
   The work required for the preceding research contributions also highlighted
the many nuances of secure speculation schemes and their details, and how
undiscovered limitations can meaningfully affect the performance and design
impact of a scheme. Our next avenue of research took a deeper dive into how
to realize secure speculation schemes, using more detailed research tools such
as hardware prototyping. This work helped uncover oversights in previous
research works and paint a more nuanced picture of the potential costs of
some secure speculation schemes.
   In Paper D: ShadowBinding, we design effective microarchitectures
for two state-of-the-art in-core secure speculation schemes, Speculative Taint
Tracking (STT) [89] and Non-speculative Data Access (NDA) [80]. Through
realizing these microarchitectures using hardware protoyping, we uncover
previously unknown design limitations. STT originally proposed that tainting,
a method for dynamically tracking data dependencies, be done as part of
the already existing register renaming mechanism. We show that there
is a fundamental difference between tainting and register renaming, and
that fixing this notably impacts the performance and resource cost of STT.
Additionally, we suggest a better performing microarchitecture that delays
the tainting process until the issue stage, achieving performance and cost
benefits for higher performance systems. For NDA, we expound on the
design considerations introduced in the original work, providing additional
insights into the integration of the simple scheme. This is our fourth research
contribution: Effective microarchitectures for state-of-the-art secure



6
                                                             1.4 Thesis Outline


speculation schemes, see Section 4.4.
   Our microarchitectural designs are complemented by detailed analyses of
their power, area, and timing costs. This sort of analysis is either not typically
accessible for architectural simulators or less reliable, due to the higher level
of abstraction employed by those simulators. Power, area, and timing analysis
helps expand our understanding of secure speculation schemes notably, espe-
cially as processor core area and power are premium resources in the modern
day of processor design [20]. Furthermore, knowing the trends of these costs
for higher performance core configurations, especially for timing and perfor-
mance, is highly valuable for evaluating the feasibility of secure speculation
scheme for a specific processor architecture. In Paper D: ShadowBind-
ing, we show the performance impact across a range of core configurations,
showing that the relative penalty increases for higher performance systems.
Our results also indicate that simpler schemes can outperform more complex
schemes when including timing in the performance evaluation, even if the
more complex scheme is able to execute more instructions. This brings us
our fifth and final research contribution: Detailed analysis of the perfor-
mance and resource costs of secure speculation schemes, including
trends for more parallel systems, see Section 4.5.
   Put together, these research contributions help us better understand the
cost of secure speculation schemes, how to realize them in hardware, which
considerations are necessary to create secure designs, and how to improve
the performance of such schemes.


1.4 Thesis Outline
The rest of this thesis is structured as follows: First, we give an introduction
to concepts of microarchitectures, observability, speculative side-channel at-
tacks, and secure speculation schemes in Chapter 2 Background. Following
this, we discuss some of the nuances of evaluated threat models, trade-offs,
and optimization strategies in Chapter 3 Trade-Offs and Optimizations. In
Chapter 4 Contributions, we list our specific contributions and help highlight
the key takeaways from the papers included in this thesis, summarizing their
implications for the field of secure speculation schemes. Finally, Chapter 5
Conclusion summarizes this thesis and suggests avenues for future explo-
ration.




                                                                                7
Chapter 2
Background
In the following sections, the concepts necessary for understanding secure
speculation schemes are presented in the following order: First, we introduce
the fundamentals of computer architecture, observability, and side-channels.
Then we discuss data-dependent behavior and speculation. Afterwards, we
discuss threat models and speculative side-channel attacks. Finally, we delve
into relevant secure speculation schemes, and their methods for providing
security within processors.


2.1 Architecture
Understanding how a computer works from an architectural standpoint is
helpful when discussing notions of security within computer architecture. A
computer can be defined from its instruction set architecture (ISA), which
defines a set of operations, such as addition, multiplication, fetch from mem-
ory, etc., and an architecturally visible state, such as registers and memory.
A complete description of an ISA involves a full description of all supported
instructions, as well as the effects of each instruction on the architectural
state [33]. In theory, if we consider only the ISA, we can treat the computer
as a finite state machine (FSM), with a program being a series of instructions
which result in a given visible state. Notably, there is a well-defined given
visible state after the execution of every single instruction, not just the entire
program. That is to say, at every step of execution, after each instruction,
there is a corresponding correct architectural state.
   Fulfilling the requirements of an ISA is not necessarily complex. A sim-
plified processor can process one instruction to completion at a time, fully
committing its effects on the architectural state before starting to process an-
other instruction. Such a processor will have low instruction throughput, as
it has to fully wait for any stalls for all instructions, such as memory latency,
and because it only processes a single instruction at a time. However, an ISA
defines only what the architectural state needs to be at the specific point of



                                                                                9
Chapter 2 Background


completing the execution of an instruction, i.e., committing it. As long as
such a state is available or can be recreated, the actual implementation of
the ISA, i.e., the microarchitecture, can employ any desired optimizations,
such as parallel processing and speculation.
   This is the core of microarchitectural optimizations: While a set of rules
are defined by the ISA, the actual design is given by the microarchitecture.
Microarchitectures employ a large selection of optimizations to improve per-
formance, simplify instruction handling, and otherwise improve the properties
of a given system. Commonly, many optimizations aim to improve the per-
formance of memory, as there is a huge difference in latency for logical and
memory components, known as the memory wall [82].
   Typically, the microarchitecture is invisible to programmers, as it is not
formally part of the ISA and is aimed at improving program performance. A
few of what is commonly considered microarchitectural features are explicitly
accessible or addressed through the ISA for various reasons, mostly stemming
from practicality or optimization possibilities [83]. A common example of
this is the ability to flush cache lines from a cache level, despite caches being
a part of the microarchitecture. However, these instructions do not modify
the architectural state, and should not be a part of program correctness. We
therefore consider these instructions to be microarchitectural.


2.2 Observability & Side-Channels
Having established the foundations of what the architectural and microar-
chitectural state entail, we now discuss how this interacts with observability
and side-channels.

2.2.1 Observability
A state consists of a series of data values, which for architectural state, reside
either in the architectural registers or in memory. A state is considered
observable if its data values can be observed, either directly or indirectly.
As established, there is a correct given architectural state after any series
of instructions are executed, as defined by the operations of the ISA. A
program, which consists of a series of instructions, thereby has an associated
architectural state, which is trivially observable or visible to itself, because
the program can freely reference and use values from this state. Critically,
this state should be invisible to all other programs according to the principle
of program isolation, i.e., each program is invisible to all other programs and
correspondingly each program is unaware of other programs.



10
                                            2.2 Observability & Side-Channels


   The microarchitectural state is the state of the microarchitecture which im-
plements a given architecture. For example, high-performance architectures
typically use register renaming to remove data hazards for execution, renam-
ing architectural registers to physical registers. After executing some amount
of instructions, an architectural register will then have a well-defined data
value, and this value is physically held by a specific physical register. Physical
registers, together with structures such as branch predictors, are examples
of microarchitectural state, which are not typically part of the visible state
of a program. A program can indirectly observe some of these values during
execution, but usually cannot directly reference the microarchitectural state.
   In some systems, parts of microarchitectural state might be modifiable
or accessible through specific instructions. These types of instructions are
microarchitecturally specific by necessity, and are generally added in order to
allow programmers to improve the performance or security of their programs.
They generally do not affect program correctness and we do not consider
them part of the architecture.

2.2.2 Side-Channels
Though programs are ideally completely isolated from each other in order to
protect program data, this is not always the case. Data leakage from pro-
grams executing occurs relatively frequently due to side-channels, inadvertent
sources of information leakage which occurs as a consequence of microarchi-
tectural features or physical design characteristics [79]. We show an example
of this in Figure 2.1. We have two programs, program A and program B, in
which program B wishes to observe information about program A. If both
programs are executing on processor cores which share a cache level, program
B can flush all cache lines from this shared cache (a). Later on, after program
A has executed (b), program B can then time the latency of all cache lines
residing in the shared cache that A could have accessed (c). Program B
can then observe which of the cache lines has a lower access latency, which
must be the cache lines that program A accessed (d), thereby observing
information about program A, and breaking program isolation [32, 87].
   Side-channels serve as inadvertent methods of information leakage, which
can both be exploited to observe the execution of other programs as discussed,
or as a method of intentional information leakage [25, 54]. Both of these
methods are exploited for different speculative side-channel attacks as we will
discuss later in Section 2.6. The key observation is that microarchitectural
features can serve as an information channel that can be indirectly observed
through specific interactions. These information channels are not well-defined



                                                                              11
Chapter 2 Background

Figure 2.1: Example showing the FLUSH+RELOAD[87] attack. Information about
the memory access of program A is visible through timing cache channels.


by the architecture, but stem from the specific implementation of a given
architecture.
   In this thesis, we concern ourselves with side-channels which are observable
inside of the processor through an attacker program, i.e., our research does
not focus on side-channel research which targets physical design characteris-
tics. While attacking physical design characteristics is an interesting area of
research, it usually requires physical interaction with the system it targets,
which falls outside the scope of our research. We make some references to
relevant aspects of physical side-channels where appropriate, such as in Sec-
tion 2.3. When we refer to side-channels, we are referring to side-channels
which can be utilized through programs executing on a system, and that
do not require physical access to the system, except for in cases where we
specifically refer to physical side-channels.
   There are many side-channels within the complex microarchitectures em-
ployed by modern high-performance processors, and the discovery of potential
side-channels is an ongoing field of research. To complement this, there is re-



12
                                               2.3 Data-Dependent Behavior


search into mitigation strategies for specific side-channels, including methods
to scramble the correlation between memory addresses and cache lines [51].
Mitigating side-channels as a class is likely to be prohibitively expensive in
terms of performance, and would require radically altering the way we design
microarchitectures, such as is done for hardened security architectures [24].
   As mentioned, programs already leak information during intended execu-
tion through side-channels. We therefore make a distinction between the
specific notions of side-channel leakage and speculative side-channel leakage.
Previous works such as Speculative Taint Tracking (STT) [89] introduced
the notion of non-speculative execution and visibility. They argued that
observable data-leakage that occur as part of non-speculative, i.e., in-order
execution of a program, should not be considered when evaluating the secu-
rity of secure speculation schemes, as logically that data could trivially leak
without speculation as well. We also utilize this notion for our work. As such,
speculative side-channel leakage is considered to be data leakage which only
occurs due to speculation, whether implicit or explicit, and would not occur
if such speculation were disabled.


2.3 Data-Dependent Behavior
Having established the concept of observability, we now connect it to the
notion of data-dependent behavior, by explaining how observability correlates
with data values. We then discuss which instructions are data-dependent and
which are data-oblivious.


2.3.1 Correlated Behaviors
During execution, certain instructions will have different behaviors which are
observable through side-channels, directly or indirectly, depending on the
value of the operands of the instruction. For example, a memory instruction
will access a different cache line depending on the value for the memory
address derived from its operands, creating a visible data-dependent behavior.
Certain operations could also have indirectly observable correlation with data
values, such as floating point division, which takes a variable amount of cycles
depending on the values of the operand. Though this does not immediately
directly reveal the data value, it can still be exploited as a side channel to
leak information consistently using specific transmission methods.
   Instructions in which there is no difference in their execution, directly or
indirectly, based on their operands, are said to be data-oblivious. This is most



                                                                            13
Chapter 2 Background


common for arithmetic operations which are often implemented in a constant-
time way. These instructions will have a different outcome, i.e., resulting data
value depending on the operands, as adding different numbers together will
give different results, but the use of different operands is indistinguishable to
an observer. Which instructions are constant-time depends on the specific
microarchitecture, and can be difficult to identify [26, 37].
   We primarily discuss data-dependent behaviors for which there is a known
side-channel by which to observe the data-dependent behavior. It is often
possible for there to be a data-dependent effect on the microarchitecture,
but as there are no known ways by which to observe this effect, i.e., there
is no known side-channel, the effect is not considered data leakage. As new
side-channels are discovered with some regularity, some works take a more
comprehensive view of data-dependent leakage, and denote that any data-
dependent effect on the microarchitecture in the system is considered data
leakage, even if there currently exists no known method by which to exploit
this data. We do not use this notion, but it is valuable for certain verification
strategies as we discuss in subsection 2.7.5.
   When considering physical side-channel attacks, the complexity of deter-
mining data-dependent behavior grows considerably. The power draw and
heat development, and even the energy flux within a system, might vary
observably depending on the values of the operands, even if such differences
are not observable architecturally and microarchitecturally, and must be
observed with physical measurements [42, 57].

2.3.2 Instructions and Data-Dependent Behavior
The aforementioned distinctions of data-dependent and data-oblivious execu-
tion, as further defined by Speculative Data-Oblivious execution (SDO) [88],
are important to understand key concepts such as threat models and trans-
mitters. As such, in this section we will generalize some of the tendencies for
data-dependent behavior, though understanding that whether an operation
is data-dependent or data-oblivious will depend on the specific microarchitec-
ture, and therefore these tendencies will not apply to all systems.
   For most systems, all memory operations are data-dependent and observ-
able. For load operations, the address of the load is observable as it moves
cache lines, and this also holds for the address of store operations. The
loaded data value, i.e., the value residing at the memory address, is however
not visible, unless used for another visible operation, a notion we exploit for
Paper B: ReCon, as we will discuss in Section 4.2. The data value of a
store is generally invisible, which is important for loading and storing secrets



14
                                                              2.4 Speculation


unobserved to and from the memory system.
   Most integer operations except division are constant time and as such
are data-oblivious. Floating point operations are also usually constant-time,
and thereby data-oblivious, except division and square root, which normally
takes a variable amount of cycles to complete depending on the values of
the operands, resulting in data-dependent behavior. Bit-wise operations are
usually constant-time and thereby data-oblivious.
   Branch instructions are data-dependent as they will cause one specific path
of instruction execution depending on the operand values. The instruction
execution stream, i.e., which instructions are being executed, is typically
considered to be observable for most security evaluations. Jump and other
control instructions are usually not data-dependent, unless the control flow
redirection is relative to some operand value [37].


2.4 Speculation
Having discussed side-channels, we now focus on speculation, discussing spec-
ulation and how it interacts with the architectural state, and then discussing
how prediction can be data-oblivious for certain data types.

2.4.1 Speculation, Prediction and Architecture
Speculation occurs whenever there are multiple possible outcomes, and in-
stead of delaying execution, a prediction is made which allows the processor
to continue executing instructions based on the prediction. We show an
example of this in Figure 2.2, in which upon encountering a branching in-
struction (a), the processor predicts which path will be taken and begins
speculatively executing path A (b). This has benefits for performance, as the
processor can perform useful work while waiting for the speculation condition
to resolve, which it can commit after speculation resolves, if it was predicted
correctly (c). If a prediction was incorrect, the processor must squash all
work it performed that is wrong as a consequence of the misprediction, and
begin execution anew from the point of misprediction (d).
   Speculation and mispredictions do not affect the architectural state, as
the architectural state only needs to be correct at the point of completion,
i.e., commit, for any given instruction. Because instructions are committed
in-order, any misprediction will be resolved and squash, before any mispre-
dicted instructions can commit. Similarly, data values originating from the
misprediction will become inaccessible, as register mappings are restored
to correspond to physical registers which hold the correct value from an



                                                                            15
Chapter 2 Background



                                    A                                           A

                      Branching                                    Predict
           br         Execution                      br           Outcome

                                    B                                           B

 cution to paths A or B.                   taken, and begins executing path A.

                                    A                                           A

                       Commit                                      Squash
           br          Correct                       br           Incorrect

                                    B                                           B

 (c) Outcome 1: Prediction was correct,    (d) Outcome 2: Prediction was incorrect,

Figure 2.2: An example showing speculation stemming from encountering a branch
instruction br.


architectural viewpoint. In this way, speculation and mispredictions are ar-
chitecturally invisible. Instructions and data values that are the result of
misspeculation are said to be transient, as they are bound to be squashed.
  Speculation occurs in many different ways in a modern high-performance
processor, and both Ghost Loads [64] and STT helped highlight common
methods of speculation. The most known form of speculation comes in the
form of conditional control flow instructions such as branches, which can
redirect control flow based on some conditional evaluation. This is a form of
explicit speculation, as the processor explicitly has a conditional instruction.
Other forms of speculation are often implicit, in which the instruction itself
does not introduce conditional execution, but some part of its execution might
require special handling. A common example of this is store-load aliasing, in
which if a load has an address that matches an older store that has not been
written out, it should retrieve its data value from that store instead of from
memory. As all older store addresses might not be resolved at the point of
load issue, a memory dependency predictor predicts whether a load should
forward from a store or fetch from memory.
  In total, we concern ourselves with the four common sources of speculation
that were established by Ghost Loads, labeling them shadows, due to their



16
                                                               2.4 Speculation


speculation casting a shadow on all following instructions. Control shadows
(C-shadows) stem from control flow instructions, data shadows (D-shadows)
stem from potential store-load aliasing, exception shadows (E-shadows) stem
from instructions potentially causing exceptions, and memory shadows (M-
shadows) stem from potential memory consistency violations. All papers
in this thesis evaluate schemes using C and D-shadows, as M-shadows are
closely tied to the memory consistency model of a system, and E-shadows
have limited impact [76].

2.4.2 Data-Oblivious Predictions
Predictors are core to speculation, as they predict the conditional outcomes
that the processor will follow until the condition is resolved. For our purposes,
we consider predictors to either be static, i.e., always predicting the same
outcome, or history-based, i.e., being trained on previous outcomes and
predicting based on this with some algorithm. When we consider static
predictors, they are clearly data-oblivious, because regardless of the condition,
they will always predict the same outcome.
   However, as we will now highlight, history-based predictors can still be
oblivious with regard to speculative data, even if they cannot be oblivious
with regard to all data. History-based predictors are trained based on the
preceding outcomes of a condition, often together with associated instruction
information, such as the PC of the instruction. When considering the security
of speculative side-channel attacks specifically, we do not concern ourselves
with leakage stemming from non-speculative execution. This means that, as
long as a history-based predictor is trained based on non-speculative data,
typically by training a predictor when an instruction is committed, instead
of during execution, the predictor is unable to make decisions based on
speculative data. Logically, the predictor cannot leak speculative data if it
never receives speculative data.
   This is important, as many predictors commonly create observable effects.
For example, a branch predictor that predicts a branch outcome will typically
create a visible outcome, namely which path of execution the processor will
continue down. However, though this reveals the outcome of the branch pre-
dictor, if that predictor is trained only on non-speculative data, the outcome
of the branch predictor cannot be based on non-speculative data. As previous
branch outcomes were already visible, the branch predictor therefore just
leaks data which has already leaked. This notion is important for explaining
how branch predictors and other observable predictors can be safely used for
secure speculation schemes. Note that if the branch condition is resolved, i.e.,



                                                                             17
Chapter 2 Background


squashes are handled or a branch is committed as correct, and the branch
condition depends on speculative data, that is an example of speculative data
leakage.


2.5 Threat Models
Understanding these concepts of architecture, microarchitecture, and ob-
servability, we now delve into threat models. A threat model defines what
constitutes secret data, i.e., data which should not leak, the capabilities of
an attacker, i.e., by which means they can observe data leakage, and which
sources of speculation to defend against. A successful attack is defined as
secret data leaking, i.e., an attacker observing the secret data through an
attacker capability. Threat models differ in these definitions as different
approaches consider different attacks meaningful to defend against. A threat
model aims to clarify the expected capabilities in an abstract manner, as to
make reasoning about the general approach of security and attacks easier
and more consistent. We clarify each of these aspects.
   Secret data is data which should not leak during execution. As noted
before, some data will leak as a consequence of non-speculative execution,
and this data, by necessity, cannot be secret, as it will leak regardless of
speculation. A very common definition of secret data is data which would
not be accessible as part of non-speculative execution, i.e., the data is only
accessible during misspeculation. Some threat models additionally define
secret data as all data which would not leak during non-speculative execution
of the program.
   The capabilities of attackers defines what channels an attacker can monitor
to observe secret data leakage. Threat models range greatly in what capa-
bilities they consider the attacker to have. The least comprehensive threat
models consider only a limited selection of side-channels, typically those they
deem the most dangerous due to noise resilience and potential bandwidth.
Many threat models consider all side-channels which have been used as part
of known side-channel attacks as a potential capability, while the strictest
models consider all microarchitectural state as a side-channel, except for fully
isolated components.
   The strictest threat model thereby consider secret data as all data which
would not leak during non-speculative execution, and all microarchitectural
state as potential side-channels. This model is valuable for systems which
benefit from verification, and can be used in strategies such as those described
by Jauch et al [37].



18
                                       2.6 Speculative Side-Channel Attacks


   We define two threat models which are frequently used. The first defines
secrets as any data that would not leak during execution of a program non-
speculatively, and defines attacker capabilities as all known side-channels,
considering all speculative shadows. We refer to this threat model as Threat-
All. The second threat model defines secrets as any data that the system
can only transiently acquire, i.e., during misspeculation, and defines attacker
capabilities as all known side-channels, considering C and D-shadows. We
refer to this threat model as Threat-Speculative. These threat models
span from comprehensive for Threat-All, to more specific but important for
Threat-Speculative, and are both useful in terms of evaluating mitigation
strategies.
   The papers in this thesis have evaluated secure schemes using Threat-
Speculative. In Section 3.1, we discuss why this threat model was generally
chosen and what that means for our research.


2.6 Speculative Side-Channel Attacks
Putting together the previous topics, we now discuss speculative side-channel
attacks, how they function, and some of the particular parts of their execution.

2.6.1 Understanding Spectre Attacks
  Each Spectre attack consists of leaking secret data through side-channels
with the use of transient execution. There are three core steps to every
speculative side-channel attack:

   1. Induce transient execution, typically by mistraining a predictor

   2. During transient execution, transmit data through side-channels

   3. Recover data post-squash through the side-channels

  For example, the classic Spectre v1 attack functions by mistraining a branch
predictor, accessing data past the bounds of a data-array, transmitting the
data through the cache hierarchy by loading a specific cache line based on
the data value, and then retrieving this data value by timing all possible
cache lines that could have been loaded. While the out-of-bounds data
access was an illegal operation, because the data access only occurred during
transient execution, and the data access instruction is squashed as a part of
misprediction handling, the illegal operation is architecturally invisible. The
accessed data value is squashed when the misprediction is detected, but as a
