the data can therefore be retrieved after the squash by timing the cache lines.
When the data value is decoded in this way, it becomes architecturally visible,
and data privacy is violated. Importantly, such attacks could potentially
steal important data such as keys for encryption, or kernel data, depending
on the specifics of the system.
   We look at Figure 2.3, which consists of a simplified example of a typical
Spectre v1 attack [43], to cover a few more of the details of speculative
side-channel attacks.
   The two defined functions are straightforward: flush_array() flushes
all cache lines belonging to the flush_array, and generator() is a simple
increasing counter that is artificially constrained to have a high latency, by
flushing the data value every time it is accessed. The while loop of the
code does the mistraining, data access and data leakage. For the first 256
iterations, it correctly accesses a data element from the data_array, but on
the next iteration, though it should exit, the branch predictor mispredicts
another loop due to the high number of successive loops.
   An out of bounds data access is then performed accessing data_array[256]
and a load is performed using the value that was obtained. Afterwards, the
misprediction is detected, the secret data value is squashed, but the modifica-
tion of the cache hierarchy by the load which used the secret is not squashed.
The final for loop then times all possible cache lines which could have been
fetched by the load which used the secret, and the one cache line which has
a lower access time than memory corresponds to the secret value.
   It is worth noting that this example is partially abstract and will need to
be modified to function on a specific microarchitecture. The reason for this is
that microarchitectural optimizations such as prefetchers are likely to trigger,
and might load additional cache lines, affecting the timing results. As such,
attack code for Spectre variants commonly has a lot of indirection and extra
operations to avoid triggering optimizations, and enable the intended attack.

2.6.2 Specific Attacks
We now list the most common speculative side-channel attacks, their desig-
nation, and central attack premise, in order to discuss their mitigations later.
This list is not comprehensive, as there are more speculative side-channel
attacks and variants than the ones listed here.
  Spectre v1 [43], named bounds check bypass (BCB), functions as a uni-
versal read gadget that can steal system memory outside its assigned data
by mistraining a predictor to escape array bounds, speculatively acquiring



                                                                            21
Chapter 2 Background


secret data, and transmitting this secret data typically through a side channel
such as the cache hierarchy before the misprediction is squashed. After the
misprediction is squashed, the secret data is still observable in the cache
hierarchy and can be recovered by timing potential cache lines. Spectre v1
is a versatile attack, and many variants have been discovered [14, 16, 40, 44].
   Spectre v2 [43], named branch target injection (BTI), functions by mis-
training a shared branch target buffer (BTB), in order to have a co-located
victim process misjump to the wrong code block, and leak its own secret data.
The premise requires that an attacker process can mistrain a shared BTB
through either shared scheduling or simultaneous multi-threading. Addition-
ally, the attacker must be aware of the victim program code, and uses the
mistrained BTB to redirect victim execution to known "gadgets", code blocks
which leak data. Similar to Spectre v1, variants of Spectre v2 have been
found, which manage to avoid some mitigations [8, 48]
   Spectre v3, Meltdown [50], named rogue data cache load (RDCL),
exploits delayed exception handling to leak sensitive data. In some systems,
accessing privileged data triggers an exception, but still returns the valid
data, though it is bound to be squashed by the exception. By setting up a
long-latency misprediction, accessing privileged data, transmitting the data,
and recovering it post the initial squash, privileged data can be stolen. The
original Meltdown is often considered an implementation error, as if the
access can be detected as excepting, it could simultaneously return a dummy
value instead of the actual data value. However, other Meltdown-style attacks
have been discovered afterwards [13–15, 19].
   Spectre v4 [36], named Speculative Store Bypass (SSB), takes advantage
of store-to-load forwarding predictions within the memory unit, in order
to trigger a misprediction in which a load is predicted not to depend on
a store it actually does depend on, read stale data from memory (which
is secret), transmitting this secret data, before finally having the store-to-
load forwarding error uncovered, and the load restarted with the right data.
After correct execution is restarted, the stale data read from memory can be
extracted through timing the cache hierarchy.


2.7 Secure Speculation Schemes
We now look at different strategies for mitigating speculative side-channel
attacks through secure speculation schemes. We categorize the mitigations
strategies of secure speculation schemes in four groups: software mitigations,
invisible speculation, cleanup, and secret-blocking.



22
                                             2.7 Secure Speculation Schemes


2.7.1 Software mitigations
The first category is based on mitigation strategies which do not require
hardware modifications. Software mitigations cannot remedy the underlying
hardware features which enable speculative side-channel attacks, and as such
must modify existing software or update microcode to mitigate attacks. When
the original Spectre and Meltdown works were released, there was a scram-
ble to find mitigations against the most pervasive Spectre versions through
changes to software methods, such as improvements to compilers, libraries,
and kernels. Strategies such as retpoline [35] were introduced, which miti-
gated the Spectre v2 by changing the target prediction to not use the BTB
in certain cases, but instead rely on the Return Address Stack, preventing
some gadget attacks. Methods for more comprehensively identifying potential
Spectre gadgets and vulnerabilities in code were developed, including static
analysis tools and extensions for developer tools [56, 78].
   Previously known side-channel mitigations such as Kernel Page-Table Iso-
lation (KPTI) helped mitigate Meltdown, by isolating the page tables for
kernel level software, and user-level software [30]. KPTI was largely successful
at mitigating the original Meltdown attack, but other Meltdown variations
have since been found which manage to circumvent existing mitigations [15].
   Instructions were eventually added that block the usage of indirect branch
prediction which was commonly exploited for Spectre v2 attacks. Exam-
ples of such instructions include IBRS and IBPB, and serve as fine-grained
barriers which block BTB control from external sources. Such instructions
and more fine-grained control of micorarchitectural features allow for more
robust software mitigations, but also incur notable performance impact [14].


2.7.2 Invisible Speculation Schemes
The next category of mitigation strategy is invisible speculation. Invisible
speculation aims to close known side-channels by no longer making them
leak data. This is done by making the side-channels either unobservable, or
data-oblivious, thereby preventing data leakage. The specific side-channels
mitigated depends on the given threat model, and the discovery of new side-
channels that fall within the threat model could require updates to a given
scheme for it to remain secure.
   Notable examples of invisible speculation include schemes such as InvisiS-
pec [84], GhostMinion [5], and Delay-on-Miss [63]. Many of these strategies
focus on the cache hierarchy, as it is the most easily exploitable and robust
side-channel. Some schemes have tried to provide generalizable strategies



                                                                            23
Chapter 2 Background


that can be applied to all side-channels, including new ones.
   InvisiSpec was one of the first secure speculation schemes introduced, and
aimed to make the cache hierarchy invisible through the use of an L0 buffer
that was validated once an instruction was known to be non-speculative,
allowing memory requests by and large to be invisible. Delay-on-Miss took
advantage of the fact that hits in the L1 cache are invisible, if the cache
replacement policy is not updated, to allow loads that hit in the L1 cache to
proceed, but delaying loads that miss. GhostMinion is more comprehensive
than the other two schemes, offering a general notion of temporal order
that should theoretically ensure that speculative data-dependent execution
remains invisible. It is difficult to analyze how to apply GhostMinion’s
temporal order for all microarchitectural features.

2.7.3 Clean-up Schemes
The third category of secure speculation schemes are clean up schemes, which
clean up side-channels after misspeculation, thereby removing the secret
data. The premise is simple: When misspeculation is detected, restore
microarchitectural state to the state it was in before speculation occurred,
or otherwise to a neutral state, so that the state cannot hold potential
secrets acquired during misspeculation. The removal of secrets from the
microarchitectural state closes side-channels, but requires modifications to
the mechanisms of existing microarchitectural features.
   CleanupSpec was a notable version of this strategy, targeting the cache as
a side-channel [62]. It tracks speculative state changes to the cache, and on
a detected misspeculation, rollbacks any non-committed changes. Once the
source of a state change becomes non-speculative, it can be committed and
become permanent and visible. Later works have found shortcomings in the
rollback solutions offered by CleanupSpec, managing to create observable data-
dependent timing differences through manipulating how much state needs to
be rolled back [49]. We discuss these limitations more in subsection 3.2.4.

2.7.4 Secret-blocking Schemes
The final category of secure speculation schemes limit secret data directly. Un-
like invisible execution, secret-blocking schemes do not modify side-channels,
but instead prevent secrets from propagating to side-channels, thereby pre-
venting secret data leakage. The method by which this is achieved varies by
scheme, with some blocking the propagation of secret data altogether, while
others track secret data during execution and prevent secret propagation only



24
                                             2.7 Secure Speculation Schemes


to known side-channels.
   We go into some more detail on two secret-blocking schemes, Non-speculative
Data Access (NDA) [80], and Speculative Taint Tracking (STT) [89], as they
are particularly relevant for this thesis. NDA offers two variants, NDA-
strict and NDA-permissive, with NDA-strict targeting all data that would
not leak normally, and NDA-permissive targeted only speculative data. For
NDA-strict, data is not allowed to propagate across the speculative barrier,
preventing speculative instructions from accessing non-speculative data, and
speculative data is blocked from propagating altogether. For NDA-permissive,
only speculative data is blocked from propagating, restricting secret data at
the source.
   STT prevents secrets from propagating to known side-channels. It dynami-
cally tracks secrets through a mechanism called tainting, in which sources of
speculative data taint their destination registers, and such taints propagate
through instructions. Instructions which depend on tainted data are blocked
from executing if they are observable and data-dependent, i.e., they effect
a known side-channel. They label such known side-channels as transmit-
ters. When a taint source becomes non-speculative, its tainted data becomes
marked as secure, and instructions can execute as normal.

2.7.5 Verification and Contracts
Designing secure speculation schemes is difficult due to the high complexity
of microarchitectures, as is verifying the correct implementation of a given
secure scheme. To address this, work has been put into developing stronger
hardware-software contracts, better modeling of microarchitectural security,
and better methods for verifying security for RTL-based designs. Though
not secure speculation schemes themselves, these techniques enhance our
understanding of microarchitectural security and better enable the utilization
of secure schemes. As such, we discuss these briefly.
   Hardware-software contracts [31] aim to provide a better method for reason-
ing about the security guarantees of a given hardware system. The methods
offer insight into the necessary security guarantees for a given software pro-
gram to be secure, and then evaluates whether the mechanisms offered by
hardware satisfy this requirement. This form of modeling allows for better in-
sights into the specifics of data-dependent execution, in-order vs out-of-order
execution, and which hardware mechanisms are necessary to support certain
forms of secure computing.
   Pensieve [86] is a microarachitectural modeling tool that allows for studying
strategies for mitigating speculative side-channel attacks. The security model



                                                                             25
Chapter 2 Background


aims to serve as a microarchitectural model that allows for more systematic
security evaluations than preceding work. Though applying the method
correctly requires a high level of competence, the method shows promise
in finding weaknesses when designing secure speculation schemes. Notably,
the original work claimed to find a flaw in the temporal ordering premise of
GhostMinion, presenting a novel attack against the scheme.
   Secure-by-Construction [37] is an RTL design methodology that aims to sys-
tematically detect potential data leakage and offer counterexamples against
insecure designs. This design methodology can be used to formally verify the
security of a given design, instead of relying on manual inspection. These
methods help provide more robust secure scheme implementations, and as
highlighted in the original work, allow for more aggressive usage of optimiza-
tions without risking accidental data leakage, as the design methodology
will automatically detect insecure optimizations. We discuss the security
complications of optimization strategies in greater detail in Section 3.3.




26
Chapter 3
Trade-Offs and Optimizations
The previous chapter provided the context necessary to understand the mech-
anisms of speculative side-channel attacks and secure speculation schemes.
We now discuss trade-offs for threat models and schemes, as well as known
optimization strategies, in order to better contextualize our contributions.


3.1 Evaluated Threat Model
In Section 2.5, we introduced the notion of threat models, and how different
threat models concern themselves with different attacker capabilities, notions
of secret data, and sources of speculation. The papers that make up this
thesis have used the Threat-Speculative model. We now discuss why we
have focused on this threat model instead of the Threat-All model.
   The chosen threat model affects viable solutions to a great extent. A
more restrictive threat model requires more comprehensive solutions which
generally incur more invasive changes to existing microarchitectures and incur
greater performance costs. Therefore, there is a trade-off between which
treat models reasonably exemplify threats and can reasonably be mitigated.
Comprehensive threat models are therefore not always the better choice if
solutions for that threat model are too expensive to be adopted, especially if
the extra security offered is not needed.
   In Section 2.6, we discussed four speculative side-channel attacks. Out of
these, Meltdown can be mitigated by having illegals loads from privileged
data return dummy values, and Spectre v2 requires a victim process to
leak its own data through known gadgets. We highlight this, because while
both Spectre v1 and Spectre v4/SSB are valid attacks under the Threat-
Speculative model, Spectre v2 is only a valid attack under the Threat-
All model, as the data itself is not speculative, only the leakage method.
Mitigating all forms of speculative leakage comprehensively is expensive, and
Spectre v2 has also been successfully partially mitigated through other
strategies, such as limiting co-locating processes [59] and BTB hashing [29].



                                                                          27
Chapter 3 Trade-Offs and Optimizations


  On the other hand, universal read gadgets, such as those stemming from
Spectre v1 are potent attack tools, that are not reliant on weaknesses in
other programs. Though these attacks remain highly technical and might
be hampered by certain strategies, attacks such as Spectre v1 are more
broadly applicable and more difficult to mitigate without secure speculation
schemes than Spectre v2 and Meltdown. Similarly, mitigating the Threat-
Speculative model is less expensive in terms of resources and performance,
making potential solutions more feasible for widespread adoption.


3.2 Scheme Trade-Offs
Previously, we introduced the four categories of secure speculation schemes
that are commonly employed. We now discuss the relative danger of specula-
tive side-channel attacks, and then discuss the trade-offs offered by different
mitigation strategies. These trade-offs, such as resource costs and perfor-
mance, will vary depending on the specific architectures for which schemes
are adopted, and so we try to generalize our analysis. For widespread adop-
tion, efficient mitigation is a key motivation for current research into secure
speculation schemes.

3.2.1 Relative Severity
We first clarify that though speculative side-channel attacks meaningfully
challenge processor security, these attacks are not currently actively used in
common malware. As of June 2025, when this thesis is being written, there
remain no hard proofs of speculative side-channel attacks being used in the
wild, even as there are many proof of concepts. There are several reasons
for this: speculative side-channel attacks are highly technical, have relatively
minor impact when compared to other common software vulnerabilities, and
might be sensitive to system noise, as most working prototypes have demon-
strated Spectre attacks in idealized environments. These factors are why
the CVSS3 rating for Spectre v1 was only 5.6, a medium score [55]. For a
more thorough consideration of the factors that affect the severity rating for
a given vulnerability, consider the metrics for the CVSS4 system [18].
   However, speculative side-channel attacks should not be disregarded simply
because their severity is lower than other vulnerabilities. If these attacks
remain unmitigated, or even if specific attacks are mitigated but not the
sources, the risk of eventual exploitation increases. Speculative side-channel
attacks are also dangerous because they are difficult to detect, as they tech-
nically do not perform any illegal operations from an architectural viewpoint,



28
                                                      3.2 Scheme Trade-Offs


as previously discussed. As these attacks target hardware, it may eventually
prove difficult to offer software solutions at all against future attacks.
  Understanding these perspectives is important to be able to accurately
assess trade-offs for secure speculation schemes. Overly costly solutions are
unlikely to be adopted, even if they are comprehensive, due to the relatively
lower severity of speculative side-channel attacks. However, some solution
remains necessary, which should ideally offer limited performance impact
and resource cost, and mitigate the most exploitable and resilient vectors of
speculative side-channel attacks.

3.2.2 Software mitigations
Upon the release of Spectre and Meltdown, software patches were quickly
released which aimed to mitigate the most dangerous aspects of these attacks.
As patches could not redress the hardware vulnerabilities directly, they em-
ployed code injection workarounds to prevent vulnerable code patterns. Some
of the original patches were criticized due to their performance impact and
inelegance, with Linus Torvalds, creator of Linux, heavily disparaging Intel’s
initial solutions [75]. The performance cost for software patches varies be-
tween applications, but range from negligible to severe, with IO operations
seeming especially affected [47, 70, 73].
   Efforts have been made to limit the prevalence of data leak "gadgets", such
as those required by Spectre v2 attacks. The aforementioned retpoline
coding strategy removed many such gadgets by changing how control flow
functions for gadgets. These efforts are helpful to limit the ease of use of
Spectre v2, but attack vulnerable code patterns and not the core of the
problem, meaning that new methods of data leakage are likely to bypass these
fixes. Later works continually find methods to bypass software patches [81].
   Mitigating other speculative side-channel attacks with software patches
is more complicated, as Spectre v1 does not rely on vulnerable code in
victim programs. Some efforts have been made to further isolate processes to
prevent available shared data through sandboxing, as done by Chrome, but
these methods do not appear to consistently mitigate Spectre, as methods to
circumvent these patches have been found [1].
   Though software mitigations have had some success in mitigating some
Spectre attacks, they appear to be inadequate for other versions, and can incur
heavy performance penalties [46, 60, 73]. Because software mitigations target
specific attacks or methods rather than the source of these vulnerabilities, it
is likely that new speculative side-channel attacks and variants will require
new patches and continual effort. There is work being done to offer better



                                                                           29
Chapter 3 Trade-Offs and Optimizations


models for software-based approaches to mitigating Spectre [34], but these
often require wide-spread dedicated effort, which seems difficult to achieve for
a real world ecosystem. As such, it does not seem that software patches alone
are an adequate solution for mitigating speculative side-channel attacks.

3.2.3 Invisible Execution Schemes
Invisible execution schemes aim to preserve the performance of systems by
allowing instructions to execute as normal, but preventing side-channels from
leaking data. Many invisible execution schemes target the cache specifically,
but depending on the threat model they may also target other, less prevalent
side-channels as well.
   Invisible execution schemes have the advantage of potentially having very
limited performance impact, due to not generally limiting the execution of
instructions. The challenges for such schemes lie instead in how to efficiently
make side-channels invisible, as well as successfully identifying all relevant
side-channels. Making a side-channel invisible involves ensuring that there is
no correlation between potentially secret data and the observable behavior of
the side-channel. This can be achieved by either making the channel entirely
unobservable, such as done by InvisiSpec [84] for the cache hierarchy, or by
ensuring that operations are constant-time or otherwise data-independent,
such as done by SDO [88] for the cache hierarchy. However, the mechanisms
for achieving invisible operation can incur performance impact as well, as
InvisiSpec still reported notable slowdown after fixing an error in their original
evaluation [85].
   As modern, high-performance cores are likely to be more complex than
the systems modeled using architectural simulators for many invisible exe-
cution schemes, it is hard to know if estimated performance and resource
costs are accurate. If an invisible execution mechanism for a side-channel
makes erroneous assumptions about the inner workings of the side-channel,
the mechanism might not function as intended or might incur different perfor-
mance penalties than the ones found initially. Some strategies therefore offer
a generalized strategy, such as GhostMinion [5], that can be more broadly
applied invariant of the specifics of a system. This still leaves resource and
performance costs difficult to accurately estimate.
   Correctly identifying relevant side-channels is also complicated. As secrets
can generally propagate freely under invisible execution schemes, correctly
identifying all side-channels which leak under the evaluated threat model
becomes important, as secrets can exist many places in the system, unlike
in secret-blocking schemes. So far observed side-channels include the cache



30
                                                        3.2 Scheme Trade-Offs


hierarchy [87], program interference [9], the TLB [28], the Return Address
Stack [44], the µ-op cache [61], instruction fetch [2], and more. Though their
relative bandwidth and noise resilience differs, the breadth of options means
that it is hard to design comprehensive invisible execution schemes, such as
those addressing the Threat-All model, as each side-channel needs to be
made invisible.
   There are many proposed invisible execution schemes, and we have pre-
viously discussed InvisiSpec, Delay-on-Miss (DoM) [63], and GhostMinion
in subsection 2.7.2. These schemes can offer promising performance, espe-
cially GhostMinion, which reported a very low (0.6%) performance overhead
while considering all side-channels. GhostMinion does require substantial
modifications to the cache hierarchy, as well as how memory consistency is
handled, and it is unknown how feasible such modifications will be for modern,
high-performance cores. Earlier works have examined hardware prototypes
of an InvisiSpec-like approach using the BOOM, and found no performance
penalty, though this evaluation was very limited in scope [27]. DoM offers
much less invasive changes, but relative higher reported IPC impact.
   As Speculative Taint Tracking (STT) [89] has high relevance for this work,
we briefly discuss Speculative Data-Oblivious execution (SDO) [88]. SDO
is an invisible execution scheme extension to STT, which makes accesses to
the cache hierarchy data-oblivious. We previously discussed how prediction
can be oblivious with regard to speculative data in subsection 2.4.2, which
is exploited by SDO. Loads which are normally restricted because they risk
leaking data are predicted to have their desired cache line reside at a given
level in the cache hierarchy, and an access is then performed to that cache
level. If the desired cache line exists at that cache level or a preceding cache
level, data is returned correctly, without updating any visible state in the
cache hierarchy. Otherwise, a null operator is returned.
   As the accessed cache level is not data-dependent, but predicted, and
because the cache line read is made invisible, previously restricted loads can
now be issued freely. The null operator is necessary to hide whether the load
was successful, as the success state of the load can leak data through careful
observation of possible addresses. Once the load is no longer dependent on
speculative data, if it failed and returned a null operator, it is squashed and
execution is restarted from the load. In cases where it is successful, no further
actions are required, allowing for substantial performance benefits.
   Using predictors trained on non-speculative data in order to break the
dependency chains from speculative data, as done for SDO, is highly valuable
and a core part of Paper A: Doppelganger Loads. Though the strategy
employed by SDO is very interesting, it requires invasive changes to the cache



                                                                             31
Chapter 3 Trade-Offs and Optimizations


hierarchy, the exact scope and impact of which is currently unknown and
difficult to estimate. SDO is an example of a scheme extension where different
approaches, in this case the secret-blocking strategy of STT and the invisible
execution of SDO, combine to give better performance.


3.2.4 Cleanup schemes
Cleanup-based schemes aim to remove data leaks once misprediction has
been detected. This has advantages, in that performance penalties only occur
during the cleanup step after mispredictions are detected. If mispredictions
are rare, then the penalty will be minor, as execution outside of misprediction
handling will be unaffected by the scheme.
   Similar to invisible execution schemes, clean-up schemes must target spe-
cific side-channels in order to hide data leakage from them. This means
that it is necessary to identify all applicable side-channels for a given threat
model in order to successfully apply a cleanup scheme. For each of these
side-channels, a mechanism must exist by which the effects of the speculative
data can be removed from the side-channel once misprediction is detected.
For the data cache for example, CleanupSpec [62] tracks speculative state
changes and rollbacks these changes upon misprediction being detected.
   Most other side-channels, such as those discussed in the previous section, do
not currently have cleanup-based solutions, as far as we are aware. While the
considerations from CleanupSpec should apply to other cache-like structures,
i.e., retaining information about victim blocks and resetting replacement
information, it is unclear how to apply cleanup-based solutions to history-
based structures. Similarly, the cost of supporting cleanup is currently not
known for all side-channels. Underprovisioning the support structures risk
either stalling execution or leaking data, but overprovisioning is likely to be
expensive, especially as the cache data structures are size sensitive.
   An important detail for cleanup-based schemes is that the cleanup mech-
anism must also be data-oblivious or unobservable. For CleanupSpec, the
cleanup/rollback mechanism takes a variable amount of time to complete
depending on the amount of state which must be rolled back. An attack,
unXpec [49], correlates the amount of state that must be rolled back with
a speculative data value, and creates an observable, data-dependent timing
channel by observing how long the rollback mechanism for CleanupSpec takes.
These sorts of attacks highlight that all mechanisms within a secure spec-
ulation scheme must be data-oblivious or unobservable for a scheme to be
secure.



32
                                                       3.2 Scheme Trade-Offs


3.2.5 Secret-Blocking Schemes
Schemes which limit propagation of secrets remain a promising venture. While
other strategies require identifying all side-channels within a threat model,
secret-blocking schemes can instead identify secret sources, which might be
easier as there are generally fewer sources of such data than there are side-
channels. Similarly, modifying these limited sources might offer less invasive
changes than modifying side-channels to be invisible or data-oblivious.
   Secret-blocking schemes have the ability to be entirely in-core, i.e., they
do not require any modifications of external components such as the cache
hierarchy. Most invisible execution schemes and clean-up schemes are by
necessity modifying the cache hierarchy, as the cache is the most prevalent
side-channel for speculative side-channel attacks. Secret-blocking schemes
instead prevent secrets from affecting the cache in a data-dependent way
by preventing the data from leaving the core. Such limited design impact
makes it easier to adopt the secure speculation schemes for industry, as
implementation and verification becomes easier.
   Different secret-blocking schemes block secrets at different points during
execution, which has implications for their strengths and weaknesses. As we
discussed in subsection 2.7.4, Non-Speculative Data Access (NDA) [80] blocks
secret data at the source, preventing registers from propagating their secret
data. STT instead tracks all secret data throughout the system, blocking only
secrets from known side-channels. These two schemes offer interesting trade-
offs between potential security and performance: NDA is able to execute
fewer types of instructions during speculation than STT, but as secrets are
blocked at their source, it is harder to circumvent the security measures.
STT is able to execute more instructions, but suffers similar challenges to
invisible execution schemes in needing to comprehensively identify all known
side-channels under the threat model. New side-channels or new variants of
existing attacks might therefore allow for leakage under STT [52].
   More dynamic blocking of secrets incurs greater design complexity, which
can lower the overall performance of a system, as we elaborate on in Paper D:
ShadowBinding. However, it also allows for more potential optimizations,
as it becomes possible to combine more strategies when secrets are not overly
restricted. In addition to SDO, STT has previously been enhanced through
the use of Secure Privacy Tracking (SPT) [17], in which secrets which are
bound-to-leak, i.e., invariant of speculation they will leak, are not protected,
improving performance. SPT requires more complex tracking of both forward
and backward propagation. It is unclear how feasible such strategies are when
considering design complexity for a hardware realizable microarchitecture, as



                                                                            33
Chapter 3 Trade-Offs and Optimizations


high complexity often limits overall system performance.


3.3 Optimization Strategies
Many secure speculation schemes have noteworthy optimizations, that is,
enhancements to the original schemes that are not required, but improves
either performance or some other design aspect. In this section we discuss
some common optimization strategies, their methods, and their limitations.


3.3.1 Value Prediction
Secure speculation scheme DoM attempted to mitigate the loss of memory-
level parallelism (MLP) introduced by its limited memory accesses through
the use of value prediction [63]. Value prediction is a technique in which a
predictor is trained using instructions and their results, i.e., the value written
to the destination register of a given instruction, and when achieving high
confidence in the value either being static, i.e., consistently the same number,
or having a consistent and easily predictable pattern such as striding, a value
prediction can be issued while waiting for the instruction to resolve. This
can enable new data flow within the system, as long as the value prediction
is correct, improving parallelism.
   DoM finds a lackluster performance benefit from value prediction for their
scheme for two primary reasons: Firstly, each value prediction introduces a
new point of speculation, which can trigger squashing and thereby require
restarting execution. Secondly, the value predictor needs to be highly con-
fident in the predicted value to minimize the amount of triggered squashes,
resulting in only a low coverage, as most instructions never successfully pass
the training threshold for predictions.
   Ultimately, in a later work [65], the authors of DoM concluded that value
prediction did not fix the performance limitations introduced by the secure
scheme, namely the lack of accessible MLP. Though value prediction could
hide the lack of MLP through the use of value prediction to some extent, the
performance benefit remained minor when correctly analyzed.


3.3.2 Early Speculation Resolution
Another approach to improve performance is to minimize the amount of
speculation present during execution, as seen in Clearing the Shadows [76].
Since speculative side-channel attacks only occur when there is speculation,



34
                                                      3.3 Optimization Strategies


    resolving such speculation earlier would theoretically improve overall per-
    formance, as whatever restrictions were imposed by the secure speculation
    scheme would be accordingly lifted earlier. This approach was achieved
    through the use of hardware-software co-design, in which both hardware and
    software are synchronized to achieve some goal, in this case lifting the cause
    of load speculations as early as possible.
       This approach holds merit, as many times the ordering of loads and specula-
    tion are arbitrary, i.e., the load is not dependent on the speculation, but their
    ordering still results in the load being speculative. A simple example is a load
    and a store which are independent of each other. If the store occurs before
    the load, the load is speculative as its memory address may be aliasing with
    the memory address of the store, even if this is known at compile-time not
    to be the case. Conversely, if the load occurs before the store, the store may
    be speculative with regard to the load, but will nevertheless not be executed
    before it is non-speculative, thereby not imparting any consequences for the
    performance of the system.
       Such reordering showed great performance results for the evaluated scheme
    Delay-on-Miss, halving the performance overhead of the secure scheme. How-
    ever, HW-SW Co-Design is difficult to utilize in practice, as it typically
    requires a more tightly integrated ecosystem between software and hardware
    than is typical.
       Another work, Pinned Loads [90], employed a similar concept in order to
    remove M-shadows. M-shadows stem from potential memory consistency
    violations (MCVs), and can be a considerable source of overall speculation
    for some architectures. In Pinned Loads, two designs are suggested for a
    microarchitecture which helps pin loads earlier during execution, removing
    the ability for them to be affected by MCVs, and thereby limiting the overall
    speculation of the system. This improves performance, as instructions are
    less restricted under secure speculation schemes if there is less speculation.

    3.3.3 Invariant Execution
    Program execution can often be reduced to a flow diagram, where control
    instructions result in the execution of one pathway or another, that might
    then converge again later. Consider the following code:
1 i n t a = array_1 [ 0 ] ;
2 i f ( a > 10) {
3       a = a ∗ 2;
4 } else {
5       a = 10;
6 }




                                                                                 35
    Chapter 3 Trade-Offs and Optimizations


7   i n t b = array_2 [ 0 ] ;

       In this example, depending on the value of integer a as evaluated in the
    if instruction, one of two operations will be performed, but regardless of the
    outcome of the control instruction, the later load into integer b from array_2
    will happen. This means that the final instruction is invariant with regard
    to the control instruction and its speculation.
       InvarSpec [91] took advantage of this notion in order to analyze instructions
    and calculate at which point they became invariant with regard to preceding
    instructions, i.e., at which point regardless of the outcomes of speculation
    they would still execute with the same data operands. Instructions which
    are invariant can be safely issued without leaking any potential secrets when
    considering the threat model Threat-Speculative, as they are bound-to-
    execute as they are. This helps improve performance.
       Unfortunately, some aspects of InvarSpec have later been found to have
    security implications, with the modifications introduced by InvarSpec be-
    ing found to be exploitable in order to leak data under secure speculation
    schemes that would not leak without the optimization. Using speculative
    interference [9], Aimoniotis et al. [4] demonstrated that InvarSpec resulted
    in the invariant instructions functioning as a transmitter under certain cir-
    cumstances, breaking the secure schemes it was optimizing.




    36
Chapter 4
Contributions
With a firm understanding of the background and key developments within
speculative side-channel attacks and secure speculation schemes, we now
delve into the research contributions that make up this thesis. As previously
mentioned, this thesis is a collection of papers which consists of four works,
Paper A: Doppelganger Loads, Paper B: ReCon, Paper C: ReCon-
naissance, and Paper D: ShadowBinding. We now discuss how the works
fit with our research contributions.


4.1 A Novel Usage of Address Prediction
Secure speculation schemes introduce notable performance penalties, due to
limiting available memory-level parallelism (MLP), speculation, and other
factors. Our first contribution, stemming from the work presented in Paper
A: Doppelganger Loads, is the novel usage of address prediction in order
to help recover lost MLP.
   Secure speculation schemes such as Non-speculative Data Access (NDA),
and Speculative Taint Tracking (STT), both allow for issue of speculative
loads, as long as those loads do not depend on speculative data. If a load
depends on speculative data, it is prevented from being issued, due to its
operands not being broadcast as ready under NDA, or because tainted loads
are blocked under STT. Issuing such a load would risk leaking secret data.
   This is where the concept of a doppelganger load comes in. Instead of
issuing the load using potentially dangerous data, we issue a doppelganger
load that is created using a predicted address, instead of the real address
that depends on speculative data. In subsection 2.4.2, we discussed how
predictions can remain data-oblivious with regards to speculative data, as
long as their predictors are exclusively trained on non-speculative data. This
is exploited to securely train the address predictor for Doppelganger Loads.
   The address predictor provides a predicted address for the doppelganger
load, breaking the dependency chain between the first and the second load,



                                                                           37
Chapter 4 Contributions


enabling the second load to securely issue using a secret-independent predicted
address. The advantage of this scheme is the ability to initiate such loads
much earlier than would normally be possible, as normal secure speculation
scheme requirements would delay the second load until its operands are
safe, while a doppelganger load can issue as soon as the predicted address is
generated.
   If the predicted address is correct, the load will have been initiated much
earlier, while if the predicted address is wrong, there is no significant perfor-
mance loss, as the second load cannot be initiated anyway until its address
is generated and non-speculative.
   Address prediction is a performance-enhancing technique for secure spec-
ulation schemes that shows solid performance improvements. The specific
performance benefits of the technique will vary depending on multiple fac-
tors, most importantly the underlying secure speculation scheme and how it
interacts with MLP, as this is the main method by which Paper A: Dop-
pelganger Loads provides a performance benefit. From our findings in the
paper, we highlighted that the performance benefit using address prediction
for an unsecure baseline, i.e., an unmodified processor core, was marginal.
However, for the secure speculation schemes which introduce a constraint on
the memory system, such as NDA, STT, and Delay-on-Miss (DoM), Doppel-
ganger Loads helps restore some amount of MLP.
   Other works such as Register File Prefetching (RFP) [69], highlight that
in the right circumstances, address prediction can also provide a performance
benefit to a baseline, although this is more specific to an individual system and
scheduling latencies than our optimization is for secure speculation schemes.
   Figure 4.1 shows the microarchitecture for Doppelganger Loads, showing
how the mechanisms of address prediction integrate easily with existing pro-
cessor design. One highlight of this microarchitecture is the address predictor
and its similarity to basic strided prefetching [21]. Because cache hierarchies
are already commonly augmented with such prefetchers for the L1 cache hier-
archy, Doppelganger Loads can use this already existing hardware structure
to issue its predictions, instead of requiring an entirely separate history and
prediction table. This helps reduce the design cost of Doppelganger Loads.

     is safe to do so based on the underlying secure speculation scheme.

                                                                                                                                           4.1 A Novel Usage of Address Prediction
Chapter 4 Contributions


   Being able to optimize the performance of secure speculation schemes
without weakening the underlying security guarantee is critical for any op-
timization strategy. We demonstrate that address prediction using Doppel-
ganger Loads does not leak any new information due to only being trained
on non-speculative, visible data. This follows from our previous discussions
of data-oblivious prediction in subsection 2.4.2. As it does not weaken the se-
curity guarantee of many secure speculation schemes and incurs only limited
cost due to its efficient reuse of existing microarchitectural features, Paper
A: Doppelganger Loads has broad applicability.


4.2 A Novel Usage of Non-Speculative Data Leakage
We have previously discussed the differences between speculative and non-
speculative data leakage in subsection 2.2.2. Based on our threat model
Threat-Speculative, we note that values that leak as part of the non-
speculative execution of a program can by definition not be secrets. Logically,
it follows that values that have already leaked once during the non-speculative
execution of a program, do not need to be protected, unless the value has
been altered since the last time it leaked. We can exploit this to improve
the performance of secure speculation schemes, by removing the protections
normally enforced by such secure schemes for values which we know to have
been leaked.
   Accesses to the memory hierarchy are visible, and accessed addresses can
be observed by any actor through non-speculative side-channel attacks such
as Flush+Reload [87]. Programs commonly feature load pairs, two loads in
which the address of the second load stems from the result of the first load,
i.e., the data value the first load fetches from memory. In such load pairs,
this data value is leaked, as the memory address of the second load is visible
and equal to that value. Because the value has leaked during the execution of
the load pair, in the future, if the same value is loaded, we can disable secure
speculation scheme protections for the value, improving the performance of
the system.
   Within the context of Paper B: ReCon, these sorts of load-load pairs
“reveal” the value residing at the first loads address when the second load
is committed, as at that point it is part of the non-speculative execution
of the program. The value residing at the first loads address is considered
to be concealed if a store instruction targets the same address, as at that
point the value will be unobserved by other actors in the system. These
two properties together form the Reveal/Conceal (ReCon) paradigm, which



40
                        4.2 A Novel Usage of Non-Speculative Data Leakage


can be universally employed, in order to improve performance for secure
speculation schemes by removing the secure speculation scheme protection
for data values that have previously leaked.
   Figure 4.2 shows the central premise of ReCon in a more visual example.
Importantly, there is no association between which actor leaked the data
value and which can benefit from the value having been leaked. A program A
can leak the data value residing at memory address 0x812, and if a program
B loads the same memory address 0x812, it will see a “revealed” value, and
can disable secure scheme protections for that data value.
   In order for the removal of protections not to potentially leak secret data
values, the reveal operation must only reveal the specific data values which
have actually leaked. For most systems, this means that revealed values need
to be tracked on a word granularity level. Similarly, Paper B: ReCon only
considers the direct load-load pairs for its reveal/conceal paradigm, because
although it is technically valid to track indirect load-load pairs, i.e., load-load
pairs which have one or more intermediate operations, such chains are much
more complex.
   The ReCon paradigm has wide applications for secure speculation schemes.
Though the original evaluation considers only integrating ReCon with STT
and NDA, removing protections is also applicable to other schemes which
restrict execution or utilize other security structures. For example, for Invi-
siSpec, a revealed load does not need to be placed into the protected buffer,
as its data does not need to be protected from being potentially transmitted.
The breadth of application is one of ReCon’s main strengths.
   We also note that another advantage of ReCon is how it scales for parallel
applications. Once a data value has been revealed, it is revealed to all actors
in the system, allowing concurrent programs to share revealed values. As
such, the optimization has a positive synergy with parallelism.

     used freely without restriction by secure speculation schemes. Concealed cache lines and cache lines to-be-concealed (in store
     buffer) suffer from normal secure speculation scheme restrictions.
                                     4.3 Reveal/Conceal for Multiple Secrets


4.3 Reveal/Conceal for Multiple Secrets
Our next research contribution is about how to apply the reveal/conceal
paradigm to instructions with multiple potential secret dependencies. Often
times, an instruction which leaks data when executed might have dependen-
cies on potentially multiple sources of secret data. In order to not leak secret
data, that instruction must be blocked from executing until all of its potential
secret data sources are non-speculative. For a reveal/conceal paradigm, this
means that all sources must be either revealed, or non-speculative, before it
is safe to execute the instructions.
   This creates a potential problem when combined with STT. STT tracks
secret data through tainting, by having speculative data sources taint their
destination registers, and such taints propagating through other instruc-
tions. When an instruction has multiple taint sources, only information
about the youngest of these is preserved during the tainting process, creat-
ing the Youngest Root-of-Taint (YRoT). It is secure for STT to only track
YRoTs, because speculation resolves chronologically, i.e., a older instruc-
tion will always become non-speculative earlier or at the same time as a
younger instruction. Therefore, if there are multiple taint sources, by the
time the youngest taint is non-speculative, any older taints must also be
non-speculative.
   When a YRoT source is found to be revealed, it is immediately broadcast
as secure, allowing transmitting instructions with that YRoT to execute.
This breaks the previous concept of chronology, because a younger taint can
be declared revealed before an older taint is non-speculative. As such, using
STT and ReCon, an older taint can be transmitted if the younger taint is
revealed, breaking the underlying security premise. In Figure 4.3, we show
an example of this, in which the final load leaks the data of an older taint
source, because only the youngest taint source is tracked, which is revealed.
   To remedy this, it is necessary to track whenever there occurs a loss
of information in secret data tracking, such as when STT tracks only a
single YRoT for multiple taint sources. The corresponding output for such
an instance must be marked as having multiple secret data sources. This
mark must propagate through any further dependencies, as all subsequent
instructions also have a dependency on multiple taint sources, but are only
tracking a single source. Instructions which are marked as depending on
multiple taint sources cannot benefit from ReCon, as ReCon reveals only a
specific data source, not all potential secret data sources.
   We present a version of ReCon which accounts for this called ReConnais-
sance, and show how it interacts with STT by detailing the necessary modi-



                                                                            43
Chapter 4 Contributions


               x86 Instruction        Taint Source    YRoT     Revealed
                  BNZ EDX                   –          –          –
              MOV ECX, [0x80]               0          –          N
              MOV EBX, [0x96]               1          –          Y
           MOV AEX, [EBX + ECX]             2          1          N

Figure 4.3: Figure from Paper C: ReConnaissance showing data lakeage with
STT+ReCon. The final load instruction leaks the value at 0x80, despite it not being
safe since it has not been revealed. This happens because only a single taint source
is tracked via the YRoT, which is set to Taint Source 1 (MOV EBX, [0x96]) and has
been revealed.


fications to taint tracking to account for multiple secrets. ReConnaissance
closes the data leakage from STT+ReCon, and we evaluate its performance
impact as well as its design considerations for secure schemes in general in
Paper C: ReConnaissance.


4.4 Effective Microarchitectures for Secure Schemes
Previous works within secure speculation schemes have provided several
promising strategies for mitigating speculative side-channel attacks. These
strategies are often presented in the abstract, with simplified microarchitec-
tures, in order to preserve generalizable properties and focus on the potential
design space rather than the specifics. As the field matures, the need for
more detailed analysis becomes valuable, as industry looks to adopt promising
strategies, and desire realizable solutions.
   For Paper D: ShadowBinding, we designed effective microarchitectures
for NDA and STT, through hardware prototyping on the BOOM. These
designs provided crucial insights into the limitations of the secure specula-
tion schemes, in addition to providing more detailed insights into costs and
performance trends, which we discuss in more depth in Section 4.5. The
microarchitectural designs are provided in full in the paper, but we discuss
the key insights derived in this section.
   STT prevents the leakage of speculative data through tainting secret data,
and blocking tainted instructions which leak data from execution. However,
the originally proposed microarchitecture for STT used the register renaming
mechanism in order to implement taint tracking. Taint tracking is a mecha-
nism of dynamic information flow tracking (DIFT) [71], in which the aim is to
track the youngest load upon which a given instruction is dependent. As we
uncover in Paper D: ShadowBinding, this form of DIFT is not compatible



44
                          4.4 Effective Microarchitectures for Secure Schemes

                     p4                    1      p6   2
       Free       p1 p7         Free           p8 p9       Free    p3 p5
       List                     List                       List
         r6 mul r5, r3           r3 add r2, r1              r1 sub r7, r8

Figure 4.4: Figure from Paper D: ShadowBinding showing the difference be-
tween register renaming and taing tracking. Because taint computations are depen-
dent on source taints, a dependency chain is created.



with register renaming for one crucial reason: For register renaming, the
renamed physical register comes from an independent source, while for taint
tracking, the taint outcome depends on the source operands of an instruction.
   We show the effects of this in Figure 4.4. Register renaming can handle
same-cycle dependencies by either later fixing the stale reads from the Register
Alias Table (RAT), or by detecting conflicts and bypassing assignments, as
seen in the figure. Importantly, the physical destination register assignment
is independent of the source registers. Taint tracking however, creates a
dependency chain when multiple instructions are tainted in parallel, which
negatively impacted timings for our design. The dependency chain scales
with the number of parallel instructions being tainted, and is likely to be
restrictive for more parallel cores such as those employed in high-performance
designs.
   We provide a functional microarchitecture for register renaming-based STT,
which addresses the differences between tainting and register renaming. Addi-
tionally, we provide a microarchitecture for an issue-based STT design, which
delays tainting until later in the execution pipeline. We show this microar-
chitecture in Figure 4.5. Such a design shows how STT-like approaches can
be applied to different stages of the pipeline in order to achieve different
performance and resource results. The advantage of the STT-Issue micorar-
chitecture are multifold: There are no complex dependency chains because
same-cycle dependencies cannot by necessity occur during the issue stage,
the instructions are less likely to be tainted as taints are calculated later,

and there is no need for checkpointing, which is necessary for the register
rename-based microarchitecture.
   There are some limitations for STT-Issue as well. As instruction issue
is already complex due to scheduling, and because it becomes necessary to
back-propagate taint information, there are still significant timing restric-
tions. However, our results indicate that this penalty is less severe for more
parallel cores than STT-Rename. Another weakness is the potential waste
of issue slots. Since the taint is not calculated before an instruction is issued,
scheduled instructions are sometimes blocked, if the instruction is found to
be tainted, and is also a transmitter.
   In addition to the microarchitectures for STT, we also provide increased
insight into NDA’s microarchitecture. Because of NDA’s simplicity, its design
translates well to a realizable hardware design, and we show the limited
necessary modifications to broadcast and writeback. Additionally, we show for
both NDA and STT how tracking speculation is already partially implemented
in high-performance cores, and can be easily modified to provide the necessary
mechanisms for determining if an instruction is speculative.


4.5 Cost and Performance Analysis for Secure Schemes
As the field of secure speculation schemes matures, and industry looks to
adopt leading solutions, it becomes more important to converge on effective,
realizable mitigation strategies, and what their resource and performance
impact might be. Previous works have commonly focused more on conceptual



46
                     4.5 Cost and Performance Analysis for Secure Schemes


models for mitigations, and as such, their performance insight is limited to
evaluating the instructions-per-cycle (IPC) impact as reported by architec-
tural simulators such as gem5 [11, 53]. However, these simulators provide
inaccurate estimations of important aspects of processor design such as tim-
ing and resource costs, which will be important for evaluating which schemes
to adopt.
   In Paper D: ShadowBinding, we present the first detailed performance
analyses of STT and NDA stemming from hardware-realized microarchitec-
tures. This analysis includes both IPC and timing impact for several core
configurations, as well as area and power analysis. These analyses help nuance
our understanding of the feasibility of different secure speculation schemes, as
we can now evaluate the tradeoffs in terms of resource and performance costs
more clearly. For example, our findings challenge conventional understanding
that NDA outperforms STT, as though STT achieves a higher IPC than NDA,
when timing factors are considered, NDA has overall higher performance, as
its simpler design has no negative impact on the timing for our evaluation.
   Our analysis included IPC, timing, and performance impact for a series of
core configurations, which modeled cores with different levels of parallelism
and total performance. We found that the higher the absolute IPC of a
system was, i.e., the more parallel the system, the greater the relative IPC
and timing penalty incurred by secure speculation schemes. This might help
explain differences in reported IPC penalties incurred by the same schemes for
different evaluations, as different baseline IPC will affect the relative impact,
resulting in potentially different conclusions.
   Critically, our analysis showed a high negative impact for more parallel
systems, challenging sentiment of Spectre being solvable in-core at limited
performance costs. The relative IPC loss increases notably for more parallel
systems, being around 19-15% for STT and 26% for NDA, for the most parallel
system evaluated. Similarly, the relative timing impact for STT increases for
more parallel systems, around 15-20% in the worst case, with NDA being
unaffected. Extrapolating for leading-edge commercial processors indicates
dramatic performance loss, well over 30% for Redwood Cove-like classes of
cores. These insights fuels further research into in-core secure speculation
schemes, including further considerations for better overall solutions, as well
as for adopting optimization strategies such as those presented in Paper A:
Doppelganger Loads and Paper B: ReCon.
   In addition to IPC and timing, Paper D: ShadowBinding also con-
tributes analysis of power and area costs for the secure speculation schemes.
Although these numbers are specific for the RTL designs on the BOOM, they
provide greater insight into potential costs for such schemes. Interestingly, the



                                                                             47
Chapter 4 Contributions


two different microarchitectures for STT vary notably in area costs, with the
rename based approach requiring more area to support branch checkpointing.
Though the exact impact is difficult to estimate for leading-edge systems, our
results indicate scope and direction, and give experienced computer architects
valuable tools to analyze scheme impact for other architectures.




48
Chapter 5
Conclusion and Future Work
This thesis has contextualized the state-of-the-art for the field of speculative
side-channel attacks, and secure speculation schemes, and motivated the trade-
offs and limitations faced by current research. The key challenge is providing
meaningful solutions to speculative side-channel attacks, solutions which are
both realizable in hardware and mitigate relevant speculative side-channel
attacks, at acceptable performance and resource costs. Our research helps
answer this challenge by providing more detailed analysis from hardware-
realized designs, helping uncover limitations and better contextualize the cost
of secure speculation schemes. As the performance cost might be prohibitively
high, we present several optimization strategies which reduce the overall
negative performance impact for secure speculation schemes.
   We have shown how address prediction can be used to break dangerous
dependency chains under speculation, allowing for the safe issuing of loads
while waiting for speculation to resolve. This method, known as Doppelganger
Loads, is detailed in Paper A: Doppelganger Loads, and is compatible
with several secure speculation schemes, showing notable performance im-
provements. The strategy reuses existing microarchitectural features to limit
its overall resource cost.
   We show how the notion of non-speculative data leakage can be exploited
in order to remove secure speculation protections from data which has al-
ready leaked, improving performance. The paradigm of ReCon is detailed
in Paper B: ReCon, tracking load-load pairs and marking the associated
memory address of the source load as leaked, removing protections for the
data. Because revealed data sources only track whether a specific data el-
ement has leaked, it is necessary to be careful when applying it to secure
schemes in which an instruction might depend on secret data sources which
are not known. Paper C: ReConnaissance shows that incorrect usage of
ReCon results in data leakage, and specifies how instructions with multiple
secret dependencies need to be handled in the fixed version ReConnaissance.
   In addition to providing optimizations, we have helped deepen understand-



                                                                            49
Chapter 5 Conclusion and Future Work


ing of secure speculation costs through providing effective microarchitectures
for two state-of-the-art speculation schemes. These microarchitectures help
uncover limitations in the original designs, such as the fundamental differ-
ence between register renaming and taint tracking, and showing how this can
be fixed. Our microarchitectures also show how alternative applications of
secure schemes can achieve better performance and resource results. These
microarchitectures are detailed in Paper D: ShadowBinding, together
with detailed analysis of the relative cost of these schemes for various core
configurations. Our analysis includes categories such as power, area, IPC,
and timing, giving a more complete view of the potential cost of secure specu-
lation schemes. This analysis is based on hardware-realizable designs, which
are likely to be more indicative than evaluations which rely on more abstract
analysis tools. We show that when timing is considered, simpler schemes
with lower IPC might outperform more complex schemes with higher IPC,
challenging prevailing sentiment about leading solutions.
   Our research contributions help push the field forward towards finding
meaningful solutions which can be adopted for future microarchitecures.
Though there remains many research ventures to explore within secure spec-
ulation schemes and their optimizations, we hope that this thesis will serve
as a step towards the eventual goal of stopping Spectre from haunting us.


5.1 Future Research Avenues
The field of secure speculation schemes is not yet a decade old, and there
remains much promising future work. Industry adoption has already begun,
with systems such as Samsung Exynos 6 [29] employing a BTB hashing
strategy that hardens against cross-thread poisoning of the BTB training,
mitigating Spectre v2. Such strategies are likely to become more present,
as computer architects continue to look for efficient and robust methods to
improve system resilience against speculative side-channel attacks.
   Future investigations will have to delve deeper into the performance cost
of secure speculation schemes, and reevaluate known approaches. Moving
towards hardware-realizable microarchitectures will help offer more compara-
ble performance and resource costs for state-of-the-art solutions, and might
help uncover unknown limitations. Leading-edge technologies are particularly
interesting, such as those research tools utilized by industry, as they might
help even better contextualize strengths and weaknesses of the currently
developed secure speculation schemes.
   Ease of adoption should also be considered for future schemes, together



50
                                                 5.1 Future Research Avenues


with performance costs. As in-core secure speculation schemes remain the
most non-invasive type of scheme, outside of pure software mitigations, it
becomes important to continue work on improving the potentially lackluster
performance of such schemes. Strategies such as those suggested in Paper
A: Doppelganger Loads and Paper B: ReCon should be evaluated using
hardware prototyping, and other optimizations methods previously proposed
should be investigated further.
   It might be valuable to evaluate the feasibility of combining strategies, as
is done for STT with SDO and SPT. Previous invisible execution strategies
such as DoM and InvisiSpec should be revisited, their impact on the memory
system investigated more thoroughly, and contrasted with more comprehen-
sive mitigation strategies using memory, such as GhostMinion, which give
great insight into comprehensive mitigations.
   More detailed understanding of the characteristics of attacks, mitigations,
and optimizations, can help find better solutions. Information about the
specifics of speculative side-channel attacks, such as their resilience and band-
width, will be valuable to categorize. Simpler partial strategies such as
in-order branch resolution, in-order scheduling for variable latency instruc-
tions, and other approaches may prove valuable in helping mitigate parts of
speculative side-channel attacks.
   Ultimately, as speculative side-channel attacks continue to haunt computer
architecture, considerable effort will have to be devoted to better securing
the core of processor architectures. Such effort will have to produce realizable
microarchitectures that adequately address reasonable threat models, with
limited performance impact, both in terms of low IPC impact, and limited
timing impact, all without requiring substantial resources. Though difficult,
continual innovation within the field of secure speculation schemes and their
optimizations gives a tentative hope for meaningful answers to the questions
posed by Spectre and Meltdown, and maybe an overall upgrade to security
for computer architecture altogether.

Paper A
Doppelganger Loads: A Safe,
Complexity-Effective Optimization for
Secure Speculation Schemes
Authors:
    Amund Bergland Kvalsvik, Pavlos Aimoniotis, Stefanos Kaxiras, Mag-
     nus Själander

Published at conference:
     50th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture (ISCA)

Copyright:
    © 2023 Copyright held by authors




                                                                    63
      Paper A
Kvalsvik et al. (2023)
    Doppelganger Loads: A Safe,
 Complexity-Effective Optimization
  for Secure Speculation Schemes
  Amund Bergland Kvalsvik1 , Pavlos Aimoniotis2 , Stefanos Kaxiras2 ,
                          Magnus Själander1
1 Norwegian University of Science and Technology, Trondheim, Norway
               2 Uppsala University, Uppsala, Sweden




                               Abstract
   Speculative side-channel attacks have forced computer architects
   to rethink speculative execution. Effectively preventing microar-
   chitectural state from leaking sensitive information will be a key
   requirement in future processor design.
     An important limitation of many secure speculation schemes is
   a reduction in the available memory parallelism, as unsafe loads
   (depending on the particular scheme) are blocked, as they might
   potentially leak information. Our contribution is to show that it is
   possible to recover some of this lost memory parallelism, by safely
   predicting the addresses of these loads in a threat-model transparent
   way, i.e., without worsening the security guarantees of the underlying
   secure scheme. To demonstrate the generality of the approach, we ap-
   ply it to three different secure speculation schemes: Non-speculative
   Data Access (NDA), Speculative Taint Tracking (STT), and Delay-
   on-Miss (DoM).
     An address predictor is trained on non-speculative data, and can af-
   terwards predict the addresses of unsafe slow-to-issue loads, preload-
   ing the target registers with speculative values, that can be released
   faster on correct predictions than starting the entire load process.
   This new perspective on speculative execution encompasses all loads,
   and gives speedups, separately from prefetching.
     We call the address-predicted counterparts of loads Doppelganger
   Loads. They give notable performance improvements for the three
   secure speculation schemes we evaluate, NDA, STT, and DoM. The



                                                                            65
Paper A Doppelganger Loads: A Safe, Complexity-Effective Optimization
for Secure Speculation Schemes


     Doppelganger Loads reduce the geometric mean slowdown by 42%,
     48%, and 30% respectively, as compared to an unsafe baseline, for
     a wide variety of SPEC2006 and SPEC2017 benchmarks. Further-
     more, Doppelganger Loads can be efficiently implemented with only
     minor core modifications, reusing existing resources such as a stride
     prefetcher, and most importantly, requiring no changes to the mem-
     ory hierarchy outside the core.


1 Introduction
With the disclosure of Spectre [26] many architectures, previously considered
secure, were shown to be unsafe because speculative execution in large part
disregarded security vulnerabilities. Since their original introduction, many
variations of the attacks have been developed [2, 8, 10, 12, 35, 43, 52], and
early mitigation strategies [3, 4, 6, 7, 19, 22, 24, 25, 27, 29, 30, 38, 39, 41, 42,
48, 50, 53–57] strive to comprehensively eliminate speculative side-channel
attacks at acceptable overheads.
   Speculative attacks exploit two key properties of modern computer architec-
tures: First, they rely on transient instructions, which are instructions that
are being executed due to an incorrect prediction in the core or as part of de-
layed exception handling, and that are bound to be squashed. Transient loads
are used to access secrets, albeit only for a limited period of time. Second,
they rely on side-channels, microarchitectural features that inadvertently leak
information, either by persistent microarchitectural state after squashing or
by transmitting information to an attacker during the speculative phase. As
shown by the example in Figure A.1 (a), these two properties enable success-
ful attacks: A secret is first accessed by a speculative load and subsequently
leaked through a side-channel by a following transmitting instruction. Some
stricter threat models also consider secrets as potentially residing in registers,
i.e., a speculative load is not required to first access the secret [13, 40].
   Early works, such as InvisiSpec [53] and Ghost Loads [41], provide solutions
for mitigating the cache as a potential side-channel, but do not eliminate
many other side-channels [17, 23, 36]. Later works, such as Speculative
Taint Tracking (STT) [55], Non-speculative Data Access (NDA) [50], Delay-
on-Miss [39], DAWG [25], and others, improve on previous work by either
achieving better performance, securing more side-channels, or both. Some
of the best performing works, such as GhostMinion [3] and speculative data
oblivious (SDO) execution [54], are able to comprehensively mitigate Spectre
at low performance overheads.
